ASEAN経済は国ごとにばらつき。ベトナムは輸出や

では食料品やエネルギー価格高騰が消費の重石に。
タイでも原油高を背景にインフレ圧力が強まる
APAC
賃金改善が続く一方、個人消費の回復に弱さ。実
質消費支出は前年比減少が継続。消費者マインド

も小幅悪化し、物価高への警戒が根強い。景気動
向指数は低下したものの、基調判断は改善を維持
日
本
先週（10/5～10/12）発表の主な経済指標
米国では金融政策運営の変化と財政懸念、欧州では財政不
安が金利上昇圧力に。欧州では⾧期金利上昇による景気下
押し圧力が、ECBの追加利上げの必要性を低下させる可能性
金利・コスト上昇などの逆風下でも、設備投資意欲は旺盛。家
計の節約志向や企業間の格差は残るものの、価格転嫁・賃上
げも継続しており、日銀の想定するシナリオに沿った動き

米国：中間選挙を目前に住宅市場は軟調、アフォーダビリティへの不満が共和党への逆風に
 住宅ローン金利は足元で再び上昇
― 借入コストの負担増により、足元の住宅ローン申請件数は減少
 物価上昇が生活コストを押し上げるなか、金利上昇による住宅取得環境の悪化も家計の不満を助長する要因に
― トランプ大統領の生活費への政策対応は約7割が「期待より悪い」と評価しており、支持率は低下基調
―11月に控える中間選挙では、アフォーダビリティへの対応が引き続き主要な論点

米国：金融政策・財政の両面から米長期金利に上昇圧力
 住宅ローン金利の基準となる米国10年債利回りは9月以降水準を切り上げ、足元（10/6時点）では5.3％近傍に
― 構成要素をみると、追加利上げ観測の高まりを受け、実質予想短期金利が上昇。ウォーシュFRB議長の就任以降、フォ
ワードガイダンスの後退を背景に、経済指標への金利の感応度が高まっている可能性
― 加えて、足元ではタームプレミアムも拡大。中間選挙を控えた家計支援策への思惑などから、財政懸念が意識されてい

る模様。トランプ大統領は10/2、Medicare加入者2,000万人超への90ドルの一時金支給を発表
5

欧州：長期金利上昇を受け、一部ECB高官は追加利上げの必要性低下を指摘
6
 フランスでは国内政治・財政面の不安から長期金利が上昇。イタリア・ギリシャなど周辺の高債務国にも波及
― フランス国債の対独利回り差は急拡大し、一時欧州債務危機（2012年）以来の水準に。その後、大統領選の有力候補と
目されるルペン氏（国民連合）が財政赤字を縮小する意欲を示し、拡大はひとまず一服
―ECBによる介入（国債買い入れ等）の可能性については、複数の高官が検討段階にないとの見方を示す
 長期金利の上昇は需要を抑制。ECB高官からは追加利上げの必要性が低下するとの見方も
― エネルギー高によるインフレ圧力と金利上昇による景気下押し圧力が併存。ECBの政策判断は一段と難しくなる可能性
仏長期金利上昇の経済影響（左）と直近のECB高官発言（右）
（出所）LSEGより、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

### Page 8

7
日本：金利・コスト上昇下でも企業活動は底堅く、設備投資意欲の強さを再確認
 10月のさくらレポートではAI関連需要の拡大とその裾野の広がりを背景に、企業活動の底堅さを確認
― 同一の企業コメントに登場するテーマの組み合わせを集計した共起分析では、「金利×設備投資」の組み合わせが増加。
金利上昇への意識が強まる一方、成長・省人化投資の旺盛なニーズは継続
 賃上げや資産効果が消費を下支えする一方、物価高を背景とした消費の二極化が継続
― 企業は価格転嫁を積極化するも、消費者の節約志向が根強く、商品競争力や顧客層によって転嫁余力に格差
⇒景気・物価は強弱入り混じるも、日銀の想定するシナリオに概ね沿った展開。12月利上げ見通しは不変
企業コメントの共起関係の変化
（注） 同一の箇条書きコメントに、双方のテーマのキーワードがある場合を1件として計上。
なお、テーマ間の因果関係を意味するものではない
（出所） 日本銀行「地域経済報告」より、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成
企業コメント概要
0 10 20 30

金利 × 設備投資
価格転嫁×企業収益
AI関連 × 設備投資
中東情勢 × 価格転嫁
節約志向 × 消費・販売
7月
10月
（件）
設備投資における金利負
担、企業収益における価
格転嫁の重要性を示唆
 AI関連需要は想定を上回るペースで拡大。半導
体製造装置に加え、工作機械や光ファイバー関連
製品など幅広い分野に波及
 金利・建設コストの上昇下でも、競争力維持に必
要な投資を継続。一方、採算悪化や調達コスト増
から投資を縮小・延期する動きも
設備投資・生産
 原材料・人件費の上昇を受け、企業の価格改定は
機動化。値上げ頻度を引き上げる企業がみられる
一方、消費者の価格感応度も高まる
 価格転嫁が進み賃上げ原資を確保できる企業が
ある一方、転嫁不足から利益を削って賃上げを続
ける企業も
価格転嫁・賃金
 株高による資産効果や賃上げを背景に、高額品・
高機能家電・旅行などの需要は堅調
 一方、物価高の長期化を受け、高年層を中心に生
活防衛意識が強まる。低価格品へのシフトや値上

世界経済：エネルギー・食料の供給ショックによる負担は、所得階層や国によって格差
 生活費高騰に伴うインフレの影響は所得階層により格差
― 食料・エネルギー支出の割合が高い低所得層は負担が大。IMFは低所得層に絞った所得移転が望ましい対応と指摘
 エネルギー・食料の供給ショックによる影響は国家間でも格差
― 先進国では、エネルギー供給ショックによる消費の下押し効果は低所得層で特に大きい
― 食料品への支出割合が高い新興国・低所得途上国では、食料供給ショックによる消費の下押し効果が相対的に大きい

 タイでは過去にも大規模洪水が発生。2011年には、日系自動車企業の部材調達が滞り、日本国内生産に影響した経験も
―2011年の洪水発生時には、工場の被災や物流停滞、人員不足が発生。タイの製造業稼働率は一時30％近く低下
― 当時浸水した工業団地では日系企業約450社が被災。タイ製部品の調達難により、日本でも完成車が一時減産に

―10月頭時点では各社、大規模な設備被害や減産は見込まず、日本の部材調達への影響も限定的となる見込み
― ただし、10月中旬にかけ更なる大雨が予想されており、操業停止の長期化や被害地域の拡大には依然警戒が必要

日本：個人消費は耐久財特需が剥落。今後は良好な所得環境を背景に緩やかに拡大
12
 8月の消費活動指数は、耐久財特需の剥落などにより、前年比＋0.9％と伸びが鈍化
― 耐久財消費は春先の自動車特需の反動から減少。食品などの非耐久財も、根強い節約志向を背景に弱含みが継続
― 底堅く推移してきたサービス消費も、台風・大雨や猛暑による外出控えの影響などから拡大ペースが鈍化
 今後は良好な所得環境が消費を下支え。個人消費は緩慢なペースながら、底堅く推移する見通し
― 実質所定内給与はプラス圏の伸びを維持しており、基調的な所得環境は良好。増加基調の購買力が消費を下支え
― ただし、インフレ率の再上昇で消費の回復ペースは緩慢。マインドの改善が一服したことも回復ペースを抑える要因に

よる海外消費（アウトバウンド消費）を加えるための調整要因。アウトバウンド消費は
SNAの家計最終消費支出に含まれるが、GDPの計算上は輸入として控除される

日本：景気ウォッチャーは消費の緩やかな持ち直しを示唆も、家計・企業とも先行きには慎重
13
 家計動向（現状判断DI）は改善。9月の消費回復を示唆するも、回復ペースは緩慢
― 特にサービス関連の上昇が顕著。8月に増勢が一服したサービス消費は、大型連休や秋の行楽需要を受け持ち直し
― ただし、先行き判断DIは低下しており、個人消費全体でみれば緩やかな回復であることを示唆
 一方、企業動向は、現状・先行きとも慎重。景気回復の勢いは強くない
― 企業動向（現状判断DI）は製造業・非製造業ともに低下。AI関連など需要は堅調も、資材高などの供給制約が重石に

― 先行き判断についても、原材料高や金利上昇、中東情勢の不透明感などが重石となっている模様

か？

## Document 020: 2026-10-15_［みずほ経済インサイト］金利上昇による企業の負担増加はいつ本格化するか_eaa924d1

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026-10-15_［みずほ経済インサイト］金利上昇による企業の負担増加はいつ本格化するか_eaa924d1.pdf
Report source: Mizuho
Date: 2026-10-15
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

金利上昇による企業の負担増加は 
いつ本格化するか

○ 日銀の政策金利や、金融市場で決まる国債金利に比べ、企業が実際に負担する金利の上昇はまだ
限定的。企業の新規借入金利や負債残高全体への影響波及に時間がかかることが背景 
○ 金融機関の新規貸出金利は、概ね3カ月かけて政策金利の変化を反映する傾向。特に地域金融機関
で上昇がやや遅く、企業規模により有利子負債利子率の上昇ペースが異なる一因に 
○ 借入・社債金利の動きを踏まえると、企業の利払い負担増加は2026～2028年度に本格化し、年間
約3兆円の利益下押し要因に。これまで以上に資本効率を意識した経営が求められる

１．政策金利や国債金利が上昇するも、企業の利払い負担増加はまだ本格化せず 
日本銀行は2026年9月の金融政策決定会合で、 政策金利を1.0％から1.25％へ引き上げた。これは1995
年以来、31年ぶりの高水準である1。また、金融市場で決まる10年国債金利（いわゆる長期金利）は、
2026年9月に節目となる3％を超えた。こちらも1996年以来30年ぶりの高さであり、政策金利や国債金
利はまさに歴史的な転換点を迎えている。 
一方で、 企業が実際に負担する金利の上昇は今のところ限定的だ。 図表1は、 企業の有利子負債利子
率 （＝支払利息等÷有利子負債残高） を指標金利 （政策金利、10年国債金利）と比較したものである。
図表 1 企業の有利子負債利子率と指標金利 図表2 企業の資金繰り判断 DI

（注） 有利子負債利子率は、 支払利息等を有利子負債 （短期 ・

### Page 2

2

図中の凡例にある数字は、 各金利についてマイナス金利政策解除 （2024年3月19日 ）前 の2023年10～12
月期と、直近の2026年4～6月期を比較した値だ。政策金利が＋0.93％Pt、10年国債金利が＋1.80％Pt
と大きく上昇した一方で、企業の有利子負債利子率は＋0.42％Ptと上昇幅が小さく、企業の利払い負
担の増加はまだ本格化していない。実際、日銀短観における企業の資金繰りに関する調査（図表2）を
みると、「楽である」の回答率から「苦しい」の回答率を差し引いたDIが、2021年以降プラス圏で概
ね横ばいを維持している。企業の実感としても、これまでに実施された複数回の利上げや、金融市場
における国債金利の大幅な上昇は、まだ資金調達環境の悪化につながっていないようだ。 
なぜ、こうしたギャップが生じるのか。主な要因として考えられるのは次の2点である。 
第一の要因は、指標金利と企業の新規調達金利とのタイムラグだ。政策金利などの指標金利が変化
してから、企業が新たに資金を調達する際の金利が変化するまで、少し時間がかかる。特に、企業の
有利子負債の半分強を占める金融機関からの借入において（図表3）、新規調達金利のタイムラグが生
じやすいと考えられる。 
第二の要因は、企業の新規調達金利と有利子負債残高全体の利払い負担とのタイムラグだ。企業が
新たに資金調達する際の金利が上昇しても、それが有利子負債残高全体にすぐ反映されるわけではな
い。 企業の金融機関借入金の4～5割程度が固定金利型であるほか2、 主に大企業が発行する社債の多く
が固定利付債であり、過去の低金利時代に調達した負債がまだ残っているためだ。 
本稿では、 これら2つの要因を踏まえて、政策金利・国債金利などの指標金利上昇を受けた企業の利
払い負担増加が、いつ本格的に現れるかを試算する3。 
２．金融機関からの新規借入金利は、地域金融機関で上昇ペースが遅い傾向 
まず、第一の要因（指標金利から企業の新規調達金利へのタイムラグ）の影響を検証しよう。図表
3に示したように、 企業の有利子負債には金融機関などからの借入金と、 主に大企業が発行する社債が
ある。このうち、社債を新規発行する際の金利は概ね国債金利に連動して決まり、タイムラグがほぼ

### Page 3

3

無いため、ここでの検証は金融機関などから新規に借り入れる際の金利が対象になる。 
図表4は、金融機関の新規貸出金利（金融機関からみた貸出）を、業態別に示したものだ。都市銀行
では新規貸出金利の上昇ペースが速い一方、第二地方銀行や信用金庫では政策金利の上昇に比べて新
規貸出金利の上昇ペースがやや遅い。こうした地域金融機関における金利上昇の遅れが、指標金利と
企業の新規調達金利のタイムラグに影響していると考えられる。 
金融機関の業態によって新規貸出金利の上昇ペースに差があるのは、貸出の金利形態別シェアが異
なることが一因であろう。大手行では企業向け貸出全体の約6割を占める変動金利型貸出のうち、ほと
んどが「市場金利連動」であるため、金融市場の変化による影響がすぐに反映されやすい。他方で、
地方銀行や信用金庫では変動金利型貸出のうち「短期プライムレート連動」のシェアが高い。短期プ
ライムレートとは、各金融機関が設定する企業向け貸出の最優遇金利である。政策金利の変更などを
反映して決められることが多いが、各金融機関の競争環境などによって反映時期・度合いに差が生じ
る4。これにより、第二地方銀行や信用金庫では都市銀行に比べて新規貸出金利の上昇ペースが遅くな
る傾向があると考えられる5。 
こうした金融機関業態別の違いは、企業サイドにおいて、有利子負債利子率の上昇ペースが規模別
に異なる一因になっている可能性がある。足元の金利上昇局面では、都市銀行からの借入が多いと考
えられる大企業（資本金10億円以上）で有利子負債利子率の上昇ペースが速く、信用金庫など地域金
融機関からの借入が多いと考えられる小規模企業（同1千万円未満）で上昇ペースが遅い（図表5）。
2010年代の金利低下局面では大企業の有利子負債利子率が小規模企業を下回る傾向にあったが、足元
では完全に逆転している。大企業は社債が有利子負債残高の約3分の1を占めるため、金融機関の新規
貸出金利のみが原因とは言えないものの、金融機関業態別の違いが企業規模別の資金調達環境に影響
を及ぼしている可能性がある点は興味深い。 
では、今後日銀がさらに利上げを進めていくと、金融機関の新規貸出金利はどの程度のペースで、
どこまで上昇していくか。過去の政策金利と新規貸出金利の関係を統計的に分析すると、 政策金利1％

### Page 4

4

Ptの上昇は新規貸出金利を当月に0.31％Pt押し上げ、その後さらに3カ月間かけて当月からの累計で
0.85％Pt押し上げる傾向があることが明らかになった。日銀が今後2027 年半ばにかけて政策金利を
2.0％まで引き上げていくことを前提にすると6、金融機関の新規貸出金利（2026年8月時点で1.78％）
は2027年秋ごろにかけて2.65％程度へ上昇すると試算される （図表6） 。 マイナス金利政策の解除前と
比較すると、2％Pt弱の上昇になる計算だ。 
３．企業の利払い負担増加は今後 2028 年度にかけて本格化。 利益を年間約 3 兆円下押し 
次に、第二の要因（企業の新規調達金利と有利子負債残高全体の利払い負担とのタイムラグ）の影
響を検証しよう。金融機関の新規貸出金利や社債の新規発行金利の上昇が、それぞれの残高全体にど
の程度のペースで反映されるかを確認し、企業の利払い負担がいつ本格化に増加するか試算する。 
金融機関の新規貸出金利と貸出残高全体の金利を比較すると、今回の金利上昇局面では新規貸出金
の上昇に対して貸出金利残高全体の金利上昇がやや遅れている（図表7）。 先に述べたように、金融機
関の貸出の一部が固定金利型であり、新規貸出や借り換えに伴って新規貸出金利の上昇が残高全体へ
波及するのに時間がかかるためだ。 
両者の過去の関係を統計的に分析すると、新規貸出金利の上昇がおよそ5年程度かけて残高全体の金
利を緩やかに押し上げる傾向があるとの結果になった。 図表6で試算したように、 先行きの新規貸出金
利が2027年秋ごろにかけて2.65％程度まで上昇していくと想定すると、貸出残高全体の金利（2026年
8月時点で1.51％ ）は2027年末に2.0％程度、2028年末に2.3％程度と緩やかに上昇し、2030年代前半に
は新規貸出金利と同じ2.65％程度へ収束していく計算になる。 
同様に社債について、 新発債金利と既発債金利を比較したものが図表8だ。 新発債金利は2022年ごろ
から上昇して足元では2％台後半に達しているが、既発債金利はまだ1％強にとどまっており、金融機
関の貸出金利に比べると残高全体への反映に時間がかかりやすいことがうかがえる。なお、日本で新

### Page 5

5

規に発行される社債の平均年限は現在5～6年であり、新発債金利は概ね5 年国債金利に連動して動く
傾向があることから、先に述べたように指標金利→新発債金利のタイムラグはほとんど生じない。 
今後、社債残高が物価上昇にあわせて年間＋2％のペースで増加していくとの前提を置き、 先行きの
償還に伴う借り換えや追加的な新規発行が行われていく場合、既発債金利は2030年代初めにかけて年
間＋0.3％Pt程度の緩やかなペースで上昇していくと試算される（新発債金利は5年国債金利の先行き
予測値に連動すると想定）。7年債や10年債といった年限が長めの社債も一定の規模があることから、
新発債金利の上昇が既発債金利に反映されるまでに時間を要する結果になった。 
最後に、金融機関の貸出金利（企業からみた借入金利7）と社債金利の試算結果を用いて、企業の有
利子負債利回りへの影響を検証する。企業の有利子負債に占める借入と社債の金額シェアを先行き一
定として、 貸出金利と社債金利の変化を有利子負債利子率に反映したものが図表9だ。2023～2025年度
の有利子負債利子率の上昇ペースは年間＋0.1～＋0.2％Pt程度と緩やかだったが、2026～2028年度に
は同＋0.3～＋0.4％Ptに加速すると見込まれる。企業の有利子負債の多くを占める借入金の金利上昇
が、主な押し上げ要因だ。2029年以後は借入金の金利上昇が落ち着く一方、社債金利の上昇継続を受
け、有利子負債利子率は2030年代にかけて緩やかに3％程度の水準まで上昇していくだろう。 
このように、金利上昇による企業の利払い負担増加は今後2028年度にかけて本格化していく見込み
だ。企業全体の有利子負債残高が先行き年間＋2％のペースで拡大していくと仮定すると、 支払利息等
が年間約3兆円増加する計算であり、経常利益（金融・保険を除くベースで2025年度：124.8兆円）に
対し年間2.4％の押し下げ要因に相当する8。 
今後、有利子負債利子率が上昇していく過程では、それに見合った利益率を達成できるかが問われ
るだろう。2025年度時点では投下資本利益率（ROIC、3.70％）の有利子負債利子率（1.45％）に対す
るスプレッドが2％Ptを上回っており、ROICでみた企業の資本効率は外部資金調達コストを十分カバー
できている（図表10）9。しかし、 有利子負債利子率が今後3％程度へ上昇していくと、ROICが現在の水
準のままではスプレッドがコロナ禍初期の2020年度並みに縮小し、企業の利払い負担に対する利益の
図表 9 企業の有利子負債利子率の試算 図表 10 有利子負債利子率と投下資本利益率

残高と社債残高のシェアを一定として、有利子負債利

みずほ総合研究所調査部（2026）「2026年・2027年 みずほ世界経済見通し ～転換点は近いのか、 投資
主導経済の行方を探る～」、2026年10月2日

1 現在の政策金利は無担保コール翌日物金利の誘導目標だが、1995 年時点では公定歩合を政策金利と見なして時系列比較してお
り、厳密には政策金利の意味するものが異なる。 
2 日本銀行「金融システムレポート」 （2026年4 月）の図表Ⅲ-1-12 によれば、法人向け貸出のうち、大手行で約40％、地域銀
行（地方銀行、第二地方銀行）や信用金庫で 45～50％程度が固定金利貸出である（2025 年 9 月末時点）。 
3 金利上昇による企業への影響には、利払いの増加によるデメリットだけでなく、利子収入の増加によるメリットもある。本稿
では、そのうちデメリットの面だけに焦点を当てて分析しており、メリットについては稿を改めて論じたい。 
4 日本銀行「金融システムレポート」 （2025年4 月）の図表Ⅵ-1-8 では、地域銀行を対象に、2024 年7 月の政策金利上昇を受け
た短期プライムレートの引き上げタイミング、引き上げ幅、引き上げ後金利の貸出先適用割合について分析が行われている。 
5 図表 4 に示した金融機関業態別の新規貸出金利には、企業向け以外の貸出も含まれる。したがって、企業向け貸出における
「市場金利連動」 、 「短期プライムレート連動」のシェアが業態別に異なることだけでなく、貸出全体に占める住宅ローン貸出
のシェアが業態別に異なることなども、新規貸出金利の上昇ペースの違いに影響していると考えられる。 
6 みずほ総合研究所調査部（2026）の見通しに基づく想定。 
7 金融機関の貸出金利には企業向け以外の貸出も含まれるが、ここでは簡易的に、企業向け貸出金利が貸出全体の金利に連動す
ると仮定して試算した。 
8 本稿では金利上昇による利払いの増加のみを計算対象としているため、利子収入の増加も考慮すれば経常利益への下押し圧力
は緩和すると考えられる。 
9 投下資本利益率（ROIC）と本来比較すべきは、負債だけでなく自己資本のコストも考慮した加重平均資本コスト（WACC）だ
が、本稿では金利上昇の影響を直接評価する観点から、投下資本利益率と有利子負債利子率を比べている。

ab 5 October 2026
Global Research
Global I/O
Smartphones
August ’26 Sell-Through: No signs of recovery
yet
Global smartphone unit sell-through -8.9% YoY August '26 YTD
Global smartphone unit sell-through (source: Counterpoint) for the month of August
'26 came in at 85.9m units (-0.1% MoM, -12.3% YoY), marking the ninth consecutive
month of YoY sell-through decline (was -12.2%/-10.9% YoY in July/June). By region, all
regions except for the US (-3.4% YoY) saw double-digit YoY declines, with India seeing

the most incremental downside at -17.7% YoY, followed by China (-17.3%), Europe (-
10.7%) and RoW (-10.1%). Following eight months of significant YoY weakness,
August '26 YTD global smartphone unit sell-through now sits at -8.9% YoY with all
regions well in negative comps (India/China/Europe/RoW/US: -12.3%/-10.5%/-8.7%/-
8.0%/-4.4% YoY).
Apple outperformance continues, Samsung beginning to rebound
Apple: YTD iPhone unit sell-through (149.3m units, +8.7% YoY) continues to highlight

the material outperformance of Apple, which remains the only major OEM (>10% unit
market share) in positive comps. With key iPhone suppliers optimistic on unit growth in
'27 (link) on the back of (1) the change in launch schedule (3 models each in 1Q27 and
3Q27) and (2) the introduction of iPhone Duo (~10m units in first 12 months), we
continue to forecast Apple strength to continue beyond '26 (UBSe sell-in: +8.5% YoY)
and into '27 (+4.7% YoY). Samsung: August '26 sell-through came in at 20.3m units

(+0.9% YoY), returning to positive comps for the first time since March '26, primarily on
the back of the release of the new flagship foldable models (Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra, Fold8,
Flip8) released on August 7th. New foldable sales in August came in at 2.8m units, only
0.3m units (or 7.5%) below July+August '25 sales for last year's lineup (Galaxy Z Fold7,
Flip7, Flip7 FE; released on July 25 th, 2025) despite two less weeks of sales. Whilst
YTD sell-through still remains soft at -4.1% YoY, we believe resilient sales of the new

flagship model as well as incremental share gains in the mid-range should help Samsung
achieve YoY unit growth (UBSe '26E sell-in: +4.4% YoY). China Android: We continue
to see memory-driven downside materialising with Transsion, Oppo, Vivo, and Xiaomi
YoY sell-through decline now at -23.1%/-19.0%/-18.2%/-18.1%.
Reiterating our sell-in forecasts of -10.0%/-3.0% YoY in '26E/'27E
We entered the year with a cautious view on the smartphone industry (link) primarily on

the back of rapidly rising memory costs. We also flagged that these dynamics will be
much more penalising for Chinese OEMs focusing on the low-end/mid-range segment.
With the DRAM/NAND upcycles likely to extend until at least 2Q28E/4Q27E and unit
sell-through now down 8.9% YoY globally (and on average 16.0% for Transsion, Oppo,
Vivo, and Xiaomi) eight months into the year, we expect to see double-digit YoY unit
decline % and reiterate our '26E sell-in forecast of -10.0% YoY. Heading into '27, with

memory likely to remain an incremental headwind, we expect to see muted demand for
smartphones and see units declining further from a low base. We recently lowered our
YoY unit sell-in forecasts for '27E to -3.0% from +2.0% (link).
Selective on smartphone plays; prefer memory/MLCC suppliers
Within the smartphone supply chain, we continue to prefer MediaTek and Samsung
(both APAC Key Call Buy) as well as ASE, Hon Hai, JCET, Micron, Murata, SEMCO, SK

Valuation Method and Risk Statement
The handset industry, as part of consumer electronics, is cyclically sensitive to the economy
and so upside and downside risks relate to macro drivers. Other downside risks include the
rapid loss of market share due to poor product timing, design or distribution. The supply
chain companies into the handset industry also operate in a highly cyclical market both from
macro concerns and inventory cycles within the supply chain. These companies also operate

in a highly competitive environment with product innovation and R&D investments critical to
success. Valuation methods across the sector include multiple analysis.

Investing in structured investments involves significant risks. For a detailed discussion of the risks involved in investing in any particular structured investment, you must read

the relevant offering materials for that investment. Structured investments are unsecured obligations of a particular issuer with returns linked to the performance of an
underlying asset. Depending on the terms of the investment, investors could lose all or a substantial portion of their investment based on the performance of the underlying
asset. Investors could also lose their entire investment if the issuer becomes insolvent. UBS does not guarantee in any way the obligations or the financial condition of any

issuer or the accuracy of any financial information provided by any issuer. Structured investments are not traditional investments and investing in a structured investment is
not equivalent to investing directly in the underlying asset. Structured investments may have limited or no liquidity, and investors should be prepared to hold their investment
to maturity. The return of structured investments may be limited by a maximum gain, participation rate or other feature. Structured investments may include call features and,

if a structured investment is called early, investors would not earn any further return and may not be able to reinvest in similar investments with similar terms. Structured
investments include costs and fees which are generally embedded in the price of the investment. The tax treatment of a structured investment may be complex and may differ
from a direct investment in the underlying asset. UBS and its employees do not provide tax advice. Investors should consult their own tax advisor about their own tax situation

before investing in any securities.
Important Information About Sustainable Investing Strategies: Sustainable investing strategies aim to consider and incorporate environmental, social and governance
(ESG) factors into investment process and portfolio construction. Strategies across geographies approach ESG analysis and incorporate the findings in a variety of ways.
Incorporating ESG factors or sustainable investing considerations may inhibit the portfolio manager’s ability to participate in certain investment opportunities that otherwise

would be consistent with its investment objective and other principal investment strategies. The returns on a portfolio incorporating ESG factors or sustainable investing
considerations may be lower or higher than portfolios where ESG factors, exclusions, or other sustainability issues are not considered by the portfolio manager, and the
investment opportunities available to such portfolios may differ.

## Document 145: Advanced economies _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Advanced economies  _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
History tends to repeat itself in advanced economies. Once again, growth ultimately fell short of expectations by only a small
margin in the first half of 2026, despite the conflict in Iran. As early as 2025, the impact of tariffs was less severe than feared.
This is a sign that structural factors (notably AI, defence and electrification) are underpinning growth in these countries

and are expected to continue to do so in 2027. However, there are significant risks to the economic outlook, primarily of a
geopolitical nature, with a significant upside risk to inflation and interest rates. The other instance of history repeating itself
concerns a period that those under 20 cannot have experienced: the period leading up to the 2008 crisis, echoes of which can
be seen in terms of growth, inflation, the direction of monetary policy, and long-term interest rates.

Advanced economies are experiencing trends similar to those they
faced nearly 20 years ago:
- Growth remains resilient in 2025-26 despite shocks, as it did in 2006-
07, in the major advanced economies.
- Inflation is above central banks’ targets: as in 2006-07, this calls for a
tightening of monetary policy.
- In Europe and the United States, long-term interest rates have re -
turned to their 2006-07 levels (1996 in Japan): a challenging environ-

ment for public finances in a context where public debt ratios are signi-
ficantly higher than those seen 20 years ago.
THE GLOBAL ECONOMY IN THE MIDST OF A GROWTH CYCLE
We are already well into 2026, and the picture so far is clear: glo -
bal growth is proving resilient. Following a US tariff shock last year,
growth is once again proving, for the time being, to be significantly less
affected by the conflict in Iran than had been anticipated when it first

broke out. Initially, however, there were fears that this new shock would
hit growth even harder. As with the tariff hike in 2025, the impact was
measurable, particularly in terms of consumer confidence. But in both
2025 and 2026, growth has also shown a degree of resilience.
The figures for the second quarter bear this out, despite the sharp rise
in oil prices, the recessionary impact of which is not yet apparent.
According to our forecasts, growth was expected to reach or exceed 1%

in 2026 in the four major advanced economies (the Eurozone, Japan,
the United Kingdom and the United States, where it exceeds 2%) for
the second consecutive year. This had not happened (excluding the
post-COVID catch-up period) since 2006-07. Growth figures are more
moderate (particularly for demographic reasons), but the private sec -
tor is not (at present) showing the same excesses (at that time, the
credit boom had preceded the worst crisis since the 1930s); although

debt levels have recently risen in the US tech sector.
The driving force behind the current growth cycle is clear: artificial
intelligence (AI). It is fuelling global trade and business investment
in most countries. This is clearly the case in the United States, where
business investment is growing strongly as investment plans in the
US tech sector are implemented.
However, the world’s major economies are currently engaged in a
broader push to expand their capital goods investment. Alongside the

ongoing development of services—particularly digital services—we
have seen a rebound in industrial production in most countries since
early 2025. The leading sectors are primarily technology equipment,
machinery, aeronautics, defence and their essential inputs (energy and
metals). This trend is evident everywhere, including in Europe, and
is becoming entrenched over the long term, with an improvement in
business climate indicators, particularly new export orders. Growth is

therefore expected to strengthen further in 2027, particularly in the
Eurozone, where German investment plans are set to gather momen -
tum and whose spillover effects will have a greater impact on the eco-
nomy.
However, not all sectors are experiencing the same momentum. This
is particularly true for the sectors that depend on household consump-
tion, which has shown mixed trends. Our observation that consumer
spending is more buoyant in the United States than in Europe was

confirmed in 2026, and the outlook does not point to a change in this
ranking.
EDITORIAL
RESILIENCE TO SHOCK AFTER SHOCK

### Page 2

4
EcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The bank
for a changing
world
While European and US households are experiencing more or less the
same energy price shock, inflation remains higher in the United States.
But this has not prevented consumer spending from keeping pace, pri-
marily because it is driven by the wealthiest households, who are less
sensitive to inflation than to the wealth effect. Furthermore, while the

savings rate remains high in the Eurozone, US households have been
drawing down their savings. This is also true of British households,
which explains why the UK has seen slightly stronger growth since
the beginning of the year. In Japan, government spending has partially
offset energy inflation (while other countries are doing so to a much
lesser extent than in 2022), which has supported consumption growth.
However, these buffers—reduced savings or fiscal support—have their

limits when shocks persist. The two major current conflicts (Iran and
Ukraine) are far from being resolved, and their impact is continuing.
This persistent geopolitical risk and its implications for inflation repre-
sent a significant headwind to the growth outlook for 2027.
HIGHER INFLATION CALLS FOR MONETARY TIGHTENING
Inflation is exceeding the target set by three of the four central banks
examined in this publication, which is another similarity (albeit with

different underlying causes) with the 2006-07 period. Japan is the ex -
ception, as government support measures for households are tempo -
rarily holding back inflation.
Apart from the Bank of Japan, which is engaged in a long-term norma-
lisation process, no central bank was expected to raise its key rates
in 2026. But they are now being prompted to do so for reasons that
vary from one region to another. The first is the conflict in Iran and
the resulting rise in oil prices, which triggered a rebound in energy

inflation from the second quarter onwards. However, since the conflict
was not resolved by the memorandum of understanding signed in June,
and given that the subsequent drop in oil prices proved short-lived,
inflation is expected to rise again. The rise in gas prices is expected to
exacerbate the impact, particularly in Europe, where inventories are
being replenished ahead of winter. Electricity prices are expected to
follow suit in most countries, but at a more moderate pace than in

2022. Indeed, gas is now used less to generate electricity than it was
at that time (thanks to the expansion of renewable energy), and to an
even lesser extent in France.
There is a significant risk that energy inflation will spread further to
other components of inflation in the future, and inflation expectations
could eventually reflect this. This is why the European Central Bank
has already raised its key interest rate twice (in June and September)

and, according to our scenario, will likely do so again in December. For
the same reason, we expect the Bank of England to raise its policy rate
in November. Although the cap on energy prices was maintained for
a long time, it has now been raised. As a result, inflation is expected
to rise between now and the end of the year. The aim will then be to
prevent second-round effects.
Inflation can also be a sign of (too much) economic strength. In the

United States, it has exceeded the 2% target since 2021. The main
argument in favour of a new cycle of rate hikes (following the one in
2022-2023) lies in the current resilience of growth and the improving
labour market. The Federal Reserve (Fed) must balance two equally
important objectives: maximum employment and price stability. Howe-
ver, unlike in the 4 th quarter of 2025, when the decline in job crea -
tion justified monetary easing (by 75 basis points), the current sharp

rebound in job creation completely reverses that analysis. According
to our forecasts, the unemployment rate is expected to decrease in
the coming months, which pushes back the prospect of a decline in
core inflation. This situation persists due to both supply constraints
(over which a central bank has no control) and excess demand (which
the Fed can, however, mitigate). Consequently, the Fed raised its key
rate in September and is expected to implement two further rate hikes

between now and January 2027.
In Japan, finally, while headline inflation has been brought down by
government support measures, core inflation remains significant.
Wage pressures are mounting against a backdrop of labour shortages.
The Bank of Japan is therefore being forced to accelerate the pace of its
monetary normalisation.
In all these regions, inflation is currently expected to peak in Q4 2026
or Q1 2027. It is against this backdrop of temporary inflation that rate

hikes could also come to an end by that time (excluding Japan). Howe-
ver, this assumes that once the energy shock—the course of which
remains uncertain—subsides, inflation will fall. The effects of El Niño,
which are still difficult to gauge at present, could take over. Inflation
would then remain above the central banks’ target for a longer period.
Under this scenario, rate hikes would go further than is currently en -
visaged.
THE RISE IN LONG- TERM INTEREST RATES: A FACTOR THAT WILL WEIGH

ON PUBLIC FINANCES
Monetary policies have also to offset the inflationary effects of fiscal
policy, particularly in the United States (where the budget deficit is
high and shows no signs of shrinking) and in Japan (where the new
government is pursuing pro-growth policies). In Europe, fiscal policy
is playing a much smaller role than it did in 2022. While the stimulus
measures implemented at that time did help reduce the inflation rate
in the short term (by nearly 2.5 pp in France, for example), by keeping

demand at a high level, they delayed disinflation and increased the
scale of the monetary tightening required.
The outbreak of the conflict in Iran acted as a catalyst: it triggered a
rise in long-term rates (which had been trending downwards at the
start of the year) to levels close to those of 2006-07. This rise is wides-
pread, but its causes vary. Inflation expectations appear to play a more
significant role in the Eurozone, whereas the rise in real interest rates

seems to be the main factor in the United States and Japan.

The bank
for a changing
world
Higher inflation and more resilient real growth than feared point in
the same direction: stronger than expected nominal growth that is li -
kely to be sustained. However, in the long term, high nominal growth
tends to drive up nominal interest rates (as it is a positive sign of more
profitable investment projects), in contrast to the problem of secular

stagnation raised in the 2010s (which combined low nominal growth
with low nominal interest rates).
The other issue is the high level of fiscal deficits (too high for a period
of growth), with various situations. In several countries, the public
debt-to-GDP ratio continues to rise. In the United States, deficit reduc-
tion does not appear to be a priority, to the extent that it is expected
to remain above 6% of GDP at the very least until the end of Donald

Trump’s term of office (with little hope that the mid-term elections will
significantly alter this trend). In the United Kingdom and France, fiscal
consolidation has begun, but it is being hampered by the simultaneous
increase in certain areas of expenditure (debt servicing, defence and
healthcare, due in particular to an ageing population), which is slowing
the pace of consolidation. In Germany, the implementation of invest -
ment plans is leading to a widening of the deficit. While this does not

pose a sustainability problem, it does result in an increase in annual
debt issuance. In Japan, the budget deficit is kept in check by interest
payments that remain low, as the rise in interest rates is recent and
the average maturity of the debt is high (10 years). However, expansio-
nary fiscal policy could pose further problems in terms of debt dyna -
mics if it were to continue through the end of the decade. In Italy and
Spain, fiscal consolidation has been sufficient to stabilise the public

debt-to-GDP ratio (and even to reduce it in Spain), but the high level of
Italy’s debt service it vulnerable to a rise in interest rates.
In all these countries, the fiscal arithmetic is simpler today than it
will be in 2030. Indeed, in most cases, only half of the debt was is -
sued after 2022 (that is, after the end of the zero-interest-rate period).
The debt service has so far only partially risen, except in the United
States, where most of the increase has already occurred due to a shor-

ter average debt maturity (6 years). This means that, for all countries
combined, the rate of GDP growth remains higher than the average
interest rate paid on public debt (or the effective interest rate).
By 2030, the effective rate will have risen and caught up with
GDP growth. As a result, in most cases, the debt service will have re -
turned to the level as a share of GDP seen in the early 2000s. In order to
stabilise public debt-to-GDP ratios, primary balances will need to have

improved. This will even require the generation of primary surpluses,
particularly in Italy (which is already achieving this), France and the
United Kingdom. This is a crucial shift that governments will need to
introduce as early as the 2027 budget; otherwise, they risk postponing
the stabilisation of their public debt-to-GDP ratio once again.

## Document 181: Australia’s Trade in Goods Account, August (2026-10-18)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Australia’s Trade in Goods Account, August (2026-10-18).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-18
Pages: 13
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#trade #australia
Australia’s Trade in Goods Account, August
By Ryan Wells
Economist, Westpac
13:19 October 01 2026 Share
Another surge in data centre-related equipment imports in August, alongside continued
volatility in gold trade. Trade balance: $0.5bn surplus. Export values: 3.7%mth. Import
values: 5.8%mth.

### Page 2

Australia’s goods trade surplus narrowed to $0.5bn in August from $1.4bn in July (revised down from
$1.9bn previously). This result was significantly lower than expected – the market consensus was for a
broadly stable reading, while Westpac had a slight widening pencilled in. Over the past six months or
so, the trade balance has been oscillating between small deficits and surpluses, largely coinciding with
significant month-to-month volatility in the trade mix. The chief culprit behind this volatility has been

trade in non-monetary gold, among other components.
Before discussing the detail, it is once again worth noting the results for gold up front. In August, non-
monetary gold exports jumped 20%, while imports surged 91%. Excluding gold, the trade figures have
been much more stable, closer to a deficit of $2-3bn per month over the past three months, down from
larger deficits around the start of the year.
Export earnings rose 3.7% in August, bouncing back from a 3.6% decline in July. The trend for exports

so far this year looks a bit more positive, but again, this is tricky to read given the volatility present in
the data. Excluding gold, export earnings were up 1.6% in August. This was largely supported by a
4.7% lift in coal and a 2.8% increase in LNG, with further detail provided by the ABS suggests that an
increase in both volumes and prices contributed. However, iron ore (–1.5%) and rural goods (–1.0%)
weighed on earnings in the month.

### Page 3

The import bill meanwhile increase by 5.8% in August. Once again, a surge in automatic data
processing (ADP) equipment imports – which capture the investment upswing related to data centres –
were in the spotlight, surging 79% in the month up to $4.3bn. There were also an increase in other
capital goods imports centred on telecommunications equipment (+12%) and civil aircraft (+30%) –
large in their own right but dwarfed by the surge in ADP imports in the month.

Our latest estimates have the total pipeline for data centres at around $175bn over the coming decade,
with much of that being front-loaded. It is natural to expect month-to-month volatility in ADP equipment
imports as these large and lumpy data centre projects are worked through, but it is worth highlighting
that August’s jump closely resembles the surge from March, when imports also surged to around
$4.3bn. Given the scale of the increase in August, Q3 is shaping up to be another quarter where data

centre investment will play a significant role in driving growth – while it is true that the data centre build-
out is import-intensive, our modelling suggests that once spending enters Australia, the local economic
impact is likely to be around half of the total investment.

### Page 4

Elsewhere across the import basket, while gold imports also surged, it was effectively offset by a
decline in consumer imports of non-industrial transport equipment (i.e. cars), down 21.2% in August
following the surge from May to July. While still tentative at this stage, it suggests that the momentum in
EV purchases, which played a significant role in the consumer spending story in Q2, may be starting to
wane. Additionally, a further pull-back in fuel imports (–5.0%) in August has extended the downward

trend in recent months, following the initial surge in oil prices in March and April after the war in the
Middle East began. However, with the conflict re-escalating recently and margins on refined fuel
products widening, we could see this trend reverse over the coming months.

## Document 220: EcoNews of 5 October 2026 (2026-10-05)

Date: 2026-10-05

ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROPEAN UNION / EUROZONE
Resilient corporate sentiment, upside inflation surprises. The Euro -
pean Commission’s Economic Sentiment Index for September showed
continued recovery in industry (+1.2 pts to -3.8, a 3.5 year high), with
a clear rebound in production and employment expectations, while

services improved further (+0.5 pt to 6.1). By contrast, household
confidence and sentiment in construction weakened further, as did
the composite index. These surveys broadly corroborate the PMIs,
with the September manufacturing PMI revised up by 0.2 pt to 52.9.
The unemployment rate held steady at a low 6.4%. Meanwhile, the
September flash inflation estimate came in above consensus at 3.8%.
The increase was driven mainly by energy (18.8% y/y, +4.5 pp) and

services (3.2%, +0.2 pp). Non-energy industrial goods inflation eased
slightly to 1.1% y/y, but the trend is firming: the 3m/3m annualised
rate is at a three-year high of 2.5%. Speaking before the data release,
ECB President Lagarde argued for a “measured response” and noted
that the increase in bond yields was delivering some of the needed
restraint. Tightening is also occurring via the cost-of-borrowing for
mortgage loans, which increased in August for the fifth consecutive

month to 3.6% (+6 bp m/m), a level unseen since September 2024.
It was unchanged for loans to non-financial corporations (3.8%).
Upcoming: ECB minutes (Thursday) and EU Trade Commissioner
Šefcovic's visit to Beijing (Thursday-Friday)
France. In the eye of the bond market storm. The government’s draft
budget for 2027, despite targeting significant adjustment (see our
detailed analysis), failed to stop the surge in the OAT yield, which
passed 4.9% on Friday morning (a high unseen since 2002), while the

OAT-bund spread reached a peak of 151 bps before easing somewhat
along with the global bond market selloff. (The peak coincided with
a statement by Bundesbank President Nagel that the ECB’s instru -
ments are focused on monetary policy transmission, not any spread
level). As expected, the draft budget law targets a deficit of 5% of GDP
(down from 5.4% in 2026), implying EUR 54 bn of savings, of which
about 2/3 coming from reduced spending. France’s independent fiscal

watchdog commended the spending moderation effort but pointed out
optimistic economic assumptions.
Meanwhile, inflation surprised on the upside: HICP inflation accele -
rated to 3.4% y/y in September (consensus: 3.1%), from 2.6% in August.
The sharp rise in energy prices (21.2%) was amplified by a 10% y/y
increase in the price of fresh food (linked to the summer drought).
Core inflation rose to 1.6% y/y in September, up from 1.3% in August
(services inflation at 2.2%; +0.3 pp m/m). The rise in industrial pro -

ducer prices accelerated to 6% y/y in August from 4.3% in July (+67%
versus +50% for refined products). Higher fuel prices also weighed on
household consumption, which fell by -0.5% m/m in August. On the
brighter side, the September manufacturing PMI was revised up by
0.3 points to 50.6.
Germany. Inflation accelerates to 3-year high. HICP inflation reached
3.3% y/y in September (vs. 2.9% in August and 3.2% expected) ac -
cording to the flash estimate (the highest since December 2023),

driven by energy prices. Core inflation rose by 2.4% (same as in Au -
gust) and services inflation edged down to 2.7% (vs. 2.8% in August).
The unemployment rate was stable in September at 6.4%.
Upcoming: August factory orders (Tuesday) and industrial production
(Wednesday)
Italy and Spain. Upside inflation surprises, business sentiment gains.
In Italy, HICP rose by 4.1% (vs. 3.2% in August), while core rose by 1.7%
(vs. 1.5% in August). In Spain, HICP inflation accelerated to 5% y/y

(vs. 4.6% in August), while core inflation reached 3.1% y/y (vs. 2.9%
earlier). Business sentiment, measured by the European Commission’s
ESI, rose slightly in both manufacturing and services in Italy, and
more significantly in Spain, where unlike in the rest of Europe, consu -
mer sentiment improved as well. Less positively, Italy’s unemploy -
ment rate unexpectedly rose to 6.2%.
UNITED KINGDOM
Bold new policy paths and upward growth revision. Prime Minister

Burnham broke several policy taboos in his Labour Party Conference
Speech, notably reconsidering Brexit and abandoning the so-called
“triple lock” guaranteeing that state pensions rise each year by the
highest of inflation, wages, or 2.5%. These proposals would be taken
up in the next Parliament. He also vowed to dismantle the legacy
of Thatcherism in public services and utilities. Real GDP growth for
Q2 2026 was revised upward to 0.5% q/q (vs. 0.4% previously, fol -

lowing 0.6% in Q1), driven by a stronger-than-expected performance
in trade and investment. Inflation expectations in the Bank of En -
gland’s Decision Maker Panel rose to 3.9% for own prices and 3.3%
for CPI one year ahead.
UNITED STATES
Solid growth, underwhelming payrolls. Q2 GDP growth was revised
up to 2.2% annualised (from 1.5%), with broad-based revisions to in -
vestment and consumption. The September ISM manufacturing in -
dex edged down (-0.1 pt to 54.5) on a drop in production. However,

new orders, a leading indicator, improved, and the employment in -
dex held at its highest level in four years. Payrolls rose by 29,000
in September, well below expectations, while the two months prior
were revised down by a combined 60,000. Job gains in healthcare
(+23k) and leisure/hospitality (+10k) were partly offset by losses in
Government (-10k). The unemployment rate rose to 4.2% (+0.1pp)
on higher labour force participation. Private hourly earnings growth

continued to slow (3% y/y, lowest since mid-2021). The August per -
sonal consumption expenditure (PCE) inflation report surprised to
the downside (3.4% vs. 3.7% expected), and core PCE was unchanged
at 3%. On the household side, the saving rate fell sharply (4.1% of
disposable income in August, from 4.6% in July), explaining the unex -
pected resilience of consumption (+0.6% in August inflation-adjusted).
All in all, this suggests the Fed will continue to raise interest rates

but doesn’t need to rush. We expect the next 25 bps hike in December.
Upcoming: September ISM services (Monday); FOMC Minutes (We -
dnesday).
JAPAN
Booming business sentiment. The Tankan survey index of large ma -
nufacturers rose to +24 (+2 pts q/q), while small and medium en -
terprises saw significant gains (+14; +5 pts q/q and +23; +5 pts q/q
respectively), driven by demand for semiconductors and production
equipment. Services sentiment weakened slightly (+35; -2 pts q/q for

large firms). Investment intentions remain strong among large cor -
porations and are showing signs of recovery in SMEs.

EMERGING ECONOMIES
Manufacturing PMIs generally stronger. The index rose to 56.7 in
Taiwan (the highest level since 2021) and 53.9 in South Korea, boosted
by strong global demand for AI goods. Korean exports increased by
a record 83.5% y/y in September (of which chips: +263% y/y), mostly
led by export prices. The manufacturing PMI also improved and was

comfortably above 50 in India, Indonesia and Thailand. It declined
slightly in Vietnam (to 51.9). Malaysia and the Philippines stood out
with PMIs falling slightly below 50. PMIs also generally improved
in central Europe. In Latin America, PMIs were more mixed: falling
sharply in Brazil to 44.8, its lowest level since early 2023, but slightly
improving in Mexico (to 50) and Colombia (to 54.6).
ASIA
China. Better outlook on small stimulus and higher PMIs. Following

a string of data showing too sluggish domestic demand to meet the
Government’s growth target of 4.5 to 5%, the State Council announced
a package of stimulus measures, including the expansion of some re -
lending facilities aimed at financing tech/innovation, agriculture and
SMEs; new spending to be financed by carried-over local govern -
ment debt quotas; and a small interest rate subsidy on mortgages.
The latest PMIs published by the NBS and by RatingDog improved

marginally, with both now showing expansion in both manufacturing
and services.
EUROPE, MIDDLE EAST
Poland. Strong uptick in September’s inflation reading. Inflation
came in at 4.0% y/y in September (the highest level since June
2025) after 3.4% y/y in August, driven by higher prices at the pump.
The increase led the government to lower its VAT rate on fuel to 8%
(23% previously) from October until year-end, which should help ease
inflationary pressures. Overall, inflation may not significantly exceed

the Central bank’s target on average in 2026. The Central Bank may
nonetheless consider one rate hike by year end.
Türkiye. A financial scandal with limited macro impact. A Ponzi sche-
me collapse has impacted around 455k retail investors with assets
reportedly worth about 1% of GDP . The crisis was swiftly handled
by the authorities to prevent financial contagion. 131 funds and two
home-finance institutions involved in the scandal were suspended

and taken over by public banks for orderly and fair disposal of their
assets. Three investment banks and 2 factoring companies were
transferred to the Savings Deposit Insurance Fund. So far, 217 people
have been subjected to legal action, of whom 90 have been jailed
pending trial. Since September 9, the BIST-30 index lost 10% but is
still 25% higher than at end-2025, and foreign investors bought equi -
ties at the highest pace in 3 months last week, suggesting a buy-the-

dip reading of the situation. There has been no or marginal spillover
effects on the exchange rate, domestic interest rates and sovereign
spreads. At this stage, contagion risk to the banking sector is very low
but the damage to small investors may have negative spillovers on
consumption through negative wealth effect. Separately, the manu -
facturing PMI fell further in September to 47.9, signaling deepening
contraction.
Saudi Arabia. Budget deficit jumps on cost of war. Budget deficit is

now forecasted at USD 65.4 bn this year (-4.9% of GDP), up from
USD 44.3 bn (-3.3% of GDP) in the initial financing bill. Revenue is
expected to be slightly higher (+3.7%) as high oil prices more than
offset the decline in oil export volume. But spending has been revised
up by 9.3%. The new growth projection is also bearish, with GDP
expected to contract by -3.6% due to the decline in oil activity while
consensus sees economy growing by +0.2%.
LATIN AMERICA

Brazil. A tight runoff, a tough policy mix inheritance. Senator Flávio
Bolsonaro and Brazil’s incumbent President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva
will face off in a runoff on October 25 th after neither won a majority
in Sunday’s general election (47% vs. 45% of the vote respectively).
Since Brazil returned to democracy in 1989, no candidate who trailed
in the first round has won the runoff. The next President will inherit
a slowing economy and a difficult policy mix: pressing ahead with

fiscal consolidation while normalising monetary policy amid recur -
ring supply shocks (adverse weather conditions, El Niño, war in the
Middle East).
ENERGY
Energy. Limited relief, electricity excepted. Re-escalation risks ap -
pear to be rising in the very short term in the Gulf and the Red Sea.
At the same time, the record high level of Gulf oil exports in Sep -
tember (above 80% of pre-war level including bypass and trade shutt-
le) has been enough to stabilize prices at a high level, albeit not to

put significant and lasting downward pressure on crude prices.
- Crude oil: prices declined slightly over the week but remain high
(-3.5% w/w to USD 101.6/b on Monday morning for Brent). The gap
with physical barrel prices (Dated Brent) is rising again USD 24.4/b on
Friday), signalling persisting tensions on the physical market.
- The diesel market got some relief from a G7 decision late on Friday
to release 100 mb of oil and diesel emergency stocks, focused on the

latter. It should take place over the next four months and reportedly
puts off the table US export restrictions. The European benchmark
fell markedly (-12% w/w to USD80/b on Monday morning). However,
market pressure could rise again soon as most Chinese refiners an -
nounced the suspension of fuel exports for October as the government
want to prioritize domestic supply security.
- EU wholesale electricity prices rose sharply in September, reaching

EUR 146/MWh on average, the highest level since Dec 2022.

## Document 234: Five ways the UK’s Autumn Budget could shock us

Source PDF: all report pdf/Five ways the UK’s Autumn Budget could shock us.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From better ﬁscal headroom to surprise income tax hikes, this
is how UK Chancellor John Healey's ﬁrst budget could surprise
investors
James Smith
UK Chancellor John Healey
In a nutshell: What we expect
Higher debt interest costs look set to drive the level of 'ﬁscal
headroom' from £24bn in March to £10bn. There's a growing
sense that Chancellor John Healey would like to get this up
to £15bn or higher. The government is set to modestly
increase spending on the cost of living, though the total

intervention is unlikely to meaningfully top £5bn and it's
likely to be mostly billed as temporary to avoid counting
against the main ﬁscal rule. We expect an increase in capital
spending of roughly £10bn/year, bearing in mind this is
exempt from the ﬁscal rule dictating that the current budget
must be in surplus after ﬁve years. To rebuild headroom

### Page 2

against that rule, we expect £5-6bn of tax hikes, likely via a
higher Bank Surcharge and possibly broadening the National
Insurance tax base.
In short, we don't expect the budget to deliver a material
stimulus (or add materially to the already-planned ﬁscal
consolidation). The impact on the Bank of England, which is
poised to hike rates in November, is likely to be fairly
minimal.
But all chancellors like to keep a rabbit in their hat – and what
follows are ﬁve ways it could surprise us.

1. Fiscal headroom goes up – not down
What if the UK’s ﬁscal hole isn’t nearly as bad as we think?
The widespread expectation going into the UK’s Autumn Budget on
28 October is that the Chancellor is going to have to face up to
higher debt interest costs with some modest tax hikes.
Bank of England rate expectations are almost a percentage point
higher than they were at the time of the Oﬃce for Budget
Responsibility’s March assessment of Britain’s public ﬁnances. 10-

year bond yields are some 70 basis points higher, which, taken
together, will push up debt interest spending by £11bn/year by
2029/30.
That points to a sizeable reduction in the amount of money the
government has left under its main ﬁscal rule, which requires the
current budget (day-to-day spending, including debt interest less
revenue) to be in surplus within three years. Back in March, that
surplus, or ﬁscal headroom, was projected at £24bn in 2029/30.

Debt interest costs are rising

We think that headroom will have dropped to around £10bn before
any corrective action. And the vibe from the Treasury suggests the
Chancellor would prefer it to be above £15bn, to give more
breathing space in case of future adverse forecast revisions. That
would point to some limited tax hikes.
But what if that’s all wrong? The truth is, nobody really knows how

the OBR will change its assessment of the UK economy. And as we
saw last year, the Treasury is often inclined to talk up the doom and
gloom to open up a positive surprise on the day. 12 months ago,
the hit to headroom was considerably smaller than had been
widely expected.
This time, any positive surprise is most likely to come from wage
growth. Will the rise in energy prices spill into higher pay, just as it
did in 2022? We think it won’t, not least because the jobs market is

much weaker, an assessment we suspect the OBR will share.
But even relatively small upgrades to wage growth can make a big
diﬀerence to the government’s fortunes. Since 2010, the OBR has
revised the level of earnings across its ﬁve-year forecast by an
average of 1.3 percentage points in absolute terms. If it did that
now, say by lifting wage growth over the next two years by 0.6ppt
apiece, it would add £10bn to headroom, almost perfectly
oﬀsetting the hit from higher interest costs.

### Page 4

There are also reasons to think the impact of lower migration
projections, which some reports suggest could wipe another £7bn
oﬀ headroom via lower tax revenue forecasts, could be much less
than feared. The starting point for employment looks a little higher
than it did back in March. The rise in global equities this year should
also be helpful for capital gains tax projections.
Why does this all matter? Because the higher the headroom, the
less ‘need’ there is to hike taxes, and if it is signiﬁcantly higher than

expected, the more room there is to increase spending under the
ﬁscal rules.
The simple message is this: don’t judge the Autumn Budget by the
level of headroom left over.
2. Spending splurge
The problem with ‘headroom’ is not only that it’s highly sensitive to
the OBR’s economic outlook, but that the forward-looking nature of
the ﬁscal rules opens the door to a lot of creative accounting.
The impact of higher inﬂation is a case in point. This helps the

Treasury through higher receipts – both VAT and income tax. Yes, it
also pushes up the cost of indexed beneﬁts. But importantly,
departmental budgets don’t automatically increase to oﬀset the
impact of higher prices.
That opens up a classic trick from the Treasury, which is to leave
spending plans virtually unchanged in nominal terms, setting up
substantially smaller real-terms budget increases across the
medium term.
Even before the Iran War, government plans pencilled in real-terms

cuts to per capita departmental spending in FY2029. Given some
functions, like health, are ‘protected’, that implies much deeper cuts
to smaller departments.
Suﬃce it to say, that is unrealistic. Higher inﬂation will push up the
wage bill across government. It would cost £12bn to simply
maintain those March departmental spending plans in real terms.
That’s before considering the cost of ending that austerity pencilled

in for later this decade, or how the government will fund its defence
or social care ambitions.
A top-up to government departments is inevitable – the question is
when. It looks like the Treasury wants to kick the can on these tricky
budget decisions until next autumn, when it plans to release its
next Spending Review, a process of allocating money across
departments. No doubt the hope is that debt interest costs will
have fallen and the economic outlook brightened, such that there's

more space under the ﬁscal rules to make bigger spending
commitments.
That points to a very modest package of spending measures this
month, mainly targeting the cost of living. On top of the already-
announced cut to VAT on energy, we’d imagine the Treasury will
replicate last year’s budget and lower household bills by funding a
greater share of ‘policy costs’ from general taxation. This has the
added beneﬁt of lowering headline inﬂation at a particularly

sensitive moment for the Bank of England. Crucially though, the
government is likely to bill these moves as temporary, so they don’t
count under that main ﬁscal rule.
But if the economic picture really is better than feared, don’t rule
out the government going further. A decision to fully fund the UK’s
3% defence spending commitment would be a major wildcard,
though this would likely come at the expense of capital plans in
other areas of government.

Ultimately, the key thing to watch is the OBR’s assessment of how
all these policy changes aﬀect GDP growth and borrowing over the
next year. If there’s a material stimulus eﬀect, it could add to
market bets on UK rate hikes.
3. A manifesto break on income tax
Tax hikes are likely at the Autumn Budget, but should be very small.
We’re probably looking at a package of circa £5bn (0.2% of GDP).
Part of the problem is that the list of viable options looks perilously

small if Labour is to avoid breaking its manifesto pledge not to raise

Is there scope for a surprise? We think there are two main ways on
tax – one good, one bad.
The good? A surprise announcement of a so-called ‘tax switch’, a
novel proposal from Resolution Foundation which would see the
government simultaneously raise basic income tax and cut

employee National Insurance by 2ppt. The impact on workers
would be neutral, but because income tax has a broader base
(including pensions), this would raise £6bn/year.
Though not a substantial sum, it would signal to investors that
Prime Minister Andy Burnham isn’t afraid to go near the thorny
issue of raising taxes. But in light of recent pension changes,

including to the infamous ‘triple lock’, this feels like too much of a
political gamble at this stage.
The negative surprise would be if the Treasury decided to raise the
basic tax allowance. Burnham ﬂirted with this on his ﬁrst day in
oﬃce; it would not only be expensive (potentially £10bn), but it
would also signal that the UK’s commitment to the threshold freeze
– which is doing the lion's share of the planned ﬁscal consolidation
– is waning.
A big rise in the National Living Wage

The minimum wage increase in April 2027, which will be
announced alongside the budget, is perhaps the most important
variable for the Bank of England.
This is based on a recommendation from the Low Pay Commission,
which earlier this year provisionally estimated the increase at 3.7%.
That’s a smaller increase than over the past two years and,
crucially, is not a million miles above the level the Bank considers
consistent with a 2% medium-term inﬂation target (3.25%).

But the LPC has some ﬂexibility in its recommendation, and one
criterion is the cost of living. Higher inﬂation means we could see a
ﬁnal proposal between 4% and 4.5%. Anything above that would,
we suspect, be problematic for the Bank of England and would
bolster the case for higher rates.
The government is no doubt well aware of this. And there appears
to be growing recognition among Labour ﬁgures, including on the
left of the party, that the big minimum wage increases, coupled

with previous payroll tax increases, have had a big impact on
business – particularly in consumer services.
But if the LPC were to recommend minimum wage growth of close
to 5%, could the government resist it? In theory, yes. But politically,
we suspect not.
A surge in bond issuance
We already know that bond issuance is set to increase again in
2027, though for reasons unrelated to the UK’s ﬁscal trajectory.

improvement in bond issuance in the medium term. That's

## Document 287: RBAは３会合ぶりの利上げ再開も、先行きは慎重姿勢 ～インフレ高止まりを警戒して追加利上げに含みもペース鈍化か､豪ドル相場はどうなる？～

Source PDF: all report pdf/RBAは３会合ぶりの利上げ再開も、先行きは慎重姿勢 ～インフレ高止まりを警戒して追加利上げに含みもペース鈍化か､豪ドル相場はどうなる？～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-09-29
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Asia Trends

2026 年 9 月 29 日

1/5 
RBA は３会合ぶりの利上げ再開も、先行きは慎重姿勢 
～インフレ高止まりを警戒して追加利上げに含みもペース鈍化か､豪ドル相場はどうなる？～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ オーストラリア準備銀行（ＲＢＡ）は９月 28～29 日の金融政策委員会で、政策金利を 25bp
引き上げて4.60％とすることを全会一致で決定した。 利上げは３会合ぶりで、 年初以降では
４回目となる。 背景には、 ７月のインフレ率が前年比＋3.5％と 13 ヵ月連続で目標レンジ （２
～３％） を上回り、 コアインフレ率も高止まりするなど、 物価上昇圧力が根強いことがある。
一方、失業率は 4.6％まで上昇したものの、雇用者数や労働参加率は高水準で、労働需給に
はなおひっ迫感が残る。 
○ ＲＢＡは声明で、イラン情勢の長期化によるエネルギー価格上昇、ＡＩ関連需要を背景とし
たテクノロジー関連財の値上がり、国内の供給制約などを理由に「インフレ率は依然として
高過ぎる」と強調した。景気や住宅市場には減速の兆しがみられるものの、インフレを適切
な期間内に目標へ戻すためには一段の金融引き締めが妥当と判断し、必要に応じて追加利上
げを行う可能性に言及した。 
○ ブロック総裁も、インフレ圧力が想定以上に長期化する可能性を指摘する一方、今後は「慎
重な引き締め」を進める姿勢を示し、インフレが落ち着けば追加利上げは不要になるとの考
えも示した。今回の利上げは市場予想通りで、声明はタカ派的だった一方、総裁会見には慎
重さもみられた。このため、米国の追加利上げ観測による米ドル高も重なり、当面の豪ドル
相場は対米ドル、対円ともに上値の重い展開が続く可能性がある。

【ＲＢＡは３会合ぶりの利上げ決定､これにより年明け以降の利上げは４回目に】 
オーストラリア準備銀行（ＲＢＡ）は、９月 28～29 日の日程で開催した定例の金融政策委員会
において、政策金利であるキャッシュレートを25bp 引き上げて4.60％とすることを決定した。Ｒ
ＢＡは、 ２月の定例会合において２年３ヶ月ぶりの利上げに舵を切り、 ５月の定例会合まで３会合
連続の利上げを実施するなど、引き締め姿勢を強めてきた。その後、６月会合、８月会合と金利を
据え置く様子見姿勢に転じたものの、今回は３会合ぶり、４回目の利上げとなる。

### Page 2

2/5 
事前の市場予想では、ＲＢＡが利上げに動くとの見方が大宗を占めた。背景には、インフレ圧力
の根強さがある。 直近７月のインフレ率
は前年比＋3.5％と前月 （同＋3.8％） か
ら鈍化したものの、13 ヵ月連続で目標
レンジ （２～３％） を上回る伸びとなっ
た（図１）。 前月比も＋1.02％と前月 （同
▲0.06％） から３ヵ月ぶりの上昇に転じ
ており、 原油高の再燃によるエネルギー
価格の上昇に加え、 生鮮品をはじめとす
る食料品価格も上昇するなど、 生活必需
品を中心にインフレ圧力が強まったこ
とが影響した。 一方で、 コアインフレ率
（トリム平均ベース） は前年比＋3.6％と前月 （同＋3.6％）から横ばいで推移したが、 こちらも10
ヵ月連続で目標レンジを上回る伸びで推移した。前月比も＋0.49％と前月（同＋0.28％）から上昇
ペースが加速しており、財・サービスともに上昇基調を強めている。月次の物価統計のなかでＲＢ
Ａが比較的注視していた物価変動の大きい財と観光を除いたベースは前年比＋3.8％と前月（同＋
4.2％） から鈍化して７ヵ月ぶりの低い伸びとなった。 しかし、 前月比は＋0.79％と前月 （同＋0.02％）
から上昇ペースが加速しており、インフレ圧力の根強さを示唆する動きもみられた。 
一方で、ＲＢＡは、物価安定と完全雇用の維持という「デュアルマンデート」に基づいた金融政
策を運営している。こうしたなか、８月の失業率（季調済）は4.6％と前月（4.5％）から0.1pt 悪
化して 2021 年 11 月以来の高水準となった（図２）。失業者数が前月比＋2.8 万人と前月（同＋0.5
万人）から３ヵ月連続で増加し、パート
タイム、フルタイム問わず求職者数が増
加したことが影響した。一方で、就業者
数は前月比＋3.9 万人と前月 （同▲1.6 万
人）から増加に転じており、フルタイム
就業者数（同▲0.6 万人）は４ヵ月ぶり
の減少に転じたものの、 パートタイム就
業者数（同＋4.6 万人）の増加が全体を
押し上げた。地域別でも、大都市部のみ
ならず、資源関連産業が集積する西オー
ストラリア州でも増加し、商品市況の上
昇が雇用環境を下支えしている。 さらに、 雇用環境の底堅さを反映して、 労働力人口は前月比＋6.8
万人、労働参加率も 67.1％と 16 ヵ月ぶりの高水準となるなど、労働市場への参入意欲の高さがう
かがえる。総労働時間は増加する一方で不完全雇用率は低下しており、 労働需給にひっ迫感が残る。
このため、金融市場ではＲＢＡによる利上げ観測が高まった。

### Page 3

3/5

【｢インフレ率が高過ぎる｣との認識をあらためて強調し､追加利上げの可能性に含み】 
会合後に公表された声明文では、今回の決定は「全会一致であった」として、すべての政策委員
が利上げを志向したことを明らかにした。そのうえで、物価動向について「 依然高水準にある」、
「８月会合時点で指摘した上振れリスクの一部が顕在化している」 との認識を示した。 具体的には
「イラン情勢が長期化し、世界のエネルギー価格は８月会合時点の想定を大幅に上回っている 」、
「ＡＩ（人工知能）関連需要を理由にテクノロジー関連財の価格も急上昇している」 、 「国内の供給
能力もひっ迫している」 ことを挙げた。 そして、 「企業部門はコスト上昇圧力に直面しており、 すで
に財・サービス価格を引き上げている、ないし引き上げを検討している」 、 「短期的なインフレ期待
も高止まりしている」として、 「足元のインフレ率は想定を上回った」とした。 
実体経済について 「生産の伸びは鈍化
しているが、４-６月はわずかに想定を
上回った」、「個人消費は想定通り緩やか
に鈍化している」 、 「大半の州都で住宅価
格は下落し（図３）、 新規住宅ローンも減
少している」とした。さらに、労働市場
について 「ここ数ヵ月はおおむね想定通
り緩和しており、 先行指標も安定してい
る」 としつつ、 「企業の設備投資と債務は
増加している」とした。先行きについて
は「不確実性が引き続き高い」として、
その理由に「イラン情勢は収束せず、インフレ率が想定を上回る一方、景気動向が想定を下回る可
能性もある」 、 「世界的な原油供給の混乱によりエネルギー価格とインフレ率に引き続き上昇圧力が
かかっている」ことを挙げた。 そして、 「不確実性が長期化すれば、 国内外の景気が一段と弱まる可
能性もある」との認識を示した。 
足元の世界経済について 「世界的なＡＩ関連投資の押し上げ効果がイラン情勢の悪影響を上回っ
ている」 とする一方、豪州経済については 「生産性の伸びの弱さが潜在成長率の重しとなっており、
住宅市場の低迷が経済に及ぼす影響にも不確実性が残る」 との見方を示した。政策運営については
引き続き「インフレが定着しないよう注力している」 としたうえで、 「そのため、 供給能力への圧力
緩和と一定期間にわたる需要抑制を通じてインフレ率を目標に戻す必要がある」 との認識を示した。
一方で、 「年初以降に実施した利上げにより金融環境は引き締まり、景気は減速している 」としつ
つ、 「インフレ率は依然として高過ぎる」 との認識を示した。今回の決定について 「足元の状況を踏
まえれば、 インフレ率を妥当な期間内に目標に収束させるには金融環境をさらに引き締めることが
妥当」との判断を示した。加えて、先行きも「インフレ率を持続的に目標水準に戻すために必要な
措置を引き続き講じる」 、 「必要であればさらなる利上げも含まれる」と追加利上げに言及した。先
行きの政策運営について「データと見通し、リスク評価を注視する」、「情勢変化に対応できる態勢
図 3 コタリティ住宅価格指数の推移

### Page 4

4/5 
は整っている」 、 「物価安定と完全雇用の実現に向けて必要な措置を講じる」 と過去数会合と同じ文
言を繰り返すなど、タカ派姿勢を維持する意向を示した。

【ブロック総裁は利上げと様子見の双方に言及､豪ドル相場の行方はどうなる？】 
会合後の記者会見に臨んだブロック総裁は、足元の物価動向について 「国内の供給能力のひっ迫
が影響している」 としたうえで、 「インフレ圧力が想定より長期化する可能性がある」 との認識を示
した。そして、 「超過需要の抑制を図る必要がある」 、 「基本シナリオでは景気後退に陥ることはな
い」との見通しを示した。今回の決定について「月次の物価統計そのものに重点を置いておらず、
金融環境を引き締める必要があると判断した」 、 「我々は金融環境が引き締め傾向にあると考える」
との見方を示しつつ、 「軽々しく決定した訳ではないが、インフレを抑えるためにやむを得ずそう
せざるを得なかった」とした。ただし、先行きについて「必要であれば利上げを実施する」としつ
つ、「慎重な引き締めを目指す」 として、 これまでの利上げペースに比べて緩やかなものとなる可能
性を示唆した。また、「物価統計の公表日に合わせて理事会の日程を調整したい」 とした。 一方で、
労働市場について「失業率は依然として歴史的低水準にある」、「雇用が失われることなく、失業率
が徐々に上昇する可能性はある」 としつつ、 「物価と賃金のスパイラル的な上昇はみられない」 とし
た。 
一方で、 「インフレが落ち着けば、 これ以上の利上げの必要性はなくなる」 、 「イラン情勢は大きな
衝撃であり、 我々を貧しくしている」 との認識も示した。 とはいえ、 「イラン情勢が長期化すればす
るほど、 企業がコストを転嫁する可能性は高まる」 として物価に与える影響を注視する考えを示し
た。足元で長期金利が上昇していることについて「債券市場は秩序ある動きをみせているが、注意
深く見守っている」 とした。 今回の協議について 「25bp の利上げか据え置きを協議した」 ことを明
らかにしたうえで、 「世界的なＡＩブームによるインフレリスクは高まりつつある 」 、 「インフレリ
スクのうち１つはすでに顕在化しており、 ２つは高まりつつある」 とした。「様々なインフレ圧力に
より極めて厳しい状況が生じている」との見方を示した。そのうえで、利上げが物価に与える影響
については「為替を通じたものになる」との認識を示した。

### Page 5

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
5/5 
このところの金融市場においては、豪ドルが強含む展開が続いてきた。日本と米国による協調介
入に加え、米国によるバイバック（長期国債の買い入れ消却）の倍増表明を受け、その後の米ドル
指数が軟調な動きを強めたため、豪ドル相場は一時２ヵ月半ぶりの高値をつけた。しかし、ＦＲＢ
（米連邦準備制度理事会）が９月の FOMC
（連邦公開市場委員会）で利上げを決定
したほか、その後も追加利上げに動くと
の観測を反映して、米ドル指数が持ち直
しの動きをみせており、豪ドル相場は足
元で下落に転じている（図４） 。 今回のＲ
ＢＡによる利上げ決定は、事前の市場予
想通りであり、声明文はタカ派姿勢の強
い内容であった。一方、ブロック総裁の
会見は、先行きの利上げ実施に含みを持
たせつつも、慎重に動く姿勢を示してお
り、声明文との間には幾分温度差があるようにみられる。このため、当面の豪ドル相場は上値の重
い展開が続く可能性があり、日本円に対しても同様の動きをみせると見込まれる。

## Document 290: S&P 500 SECTORS CALL_ More Green On The Screen

Source PDF: all report pdf/S&P 500 SECTORS CALL_ More Green On The Screen.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-10-12
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: S&P 500 SECTORS CALL: More Green On The Screen
Date:    7 hours ago
S&P 500 SECTORS CALL: More Green On The Screen

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
S&P 500 SECTORS CALL: More
Green On The Screen
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 11 Oct 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Bond yields edged down last week. As a result, the S&P 500 edged up,
led by interest-rate-sensitive sectors. The AI-related sectors Industrials
(-0.4%) and Information Technology (-0.3%) edged down and in fact were
the only down sectors last week. The big interest-rate-sensitive gainers
were Consumer Staples (4.0%), Utilities (3.9%), Consumer Discretionary
(3.1%), and Financials (2.3%).
The year's laggards were last week's leaders (chart).
Let's have a look at the Energy, Financials, and Industrials sectors:

(1) Energy. S&P 500 Energy (3.6%) powered ahead on renewed risks to
ships passing through the Strait of Hormuz (chart). Over the weekend, the
Houthis attacked King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh and declared
Saudi airspace a zone of military operations, warning airlines, travelers,
and airport workers to steer clear of airports across the kingdom.
Sector momentum turned decisively positive mid-week, bolstered by a
late-week surge as energy equities reacted to escalating Middle East

conflicts and subsequent spikes in crude oil benchmarks (chart). Brent
crude is quiet tonight at $103.65 per barrel. President Donald Trump
helped calm the oil market by announcing that an energy ceasefire
between Russia and Ukraine took effect immediately, saying on social
media that both parties had agreed to the terms.

### Page 3

The rebound in the Energy sector's forward earnings this year has been
attributable to the jump in the sector's forward profit margin, fueled by the
jump in oil prices (chart).
The sector's forward earnings is up 61.7% y/y, and its stock price index is
up 53.1% (chart). There should be more upside for the rest of the year,
especially if the war in the Middle East heats up after the mid-term
elections, as Trump has threatened.

### Page 4

Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing has been the sector's big winner so far
this year (chart). A global shortage of refinery capacity might fuel more
gains.
(2) Financials. The Q3-2026 earnings reporting season starts this week
with the big banks. The sector's stock price index is nearly down at its
200-day moving average (chart). Solid earnings reports from the banks
are likely, as we explained last week. If so, then the sector's uptrend
should hold.

### Page 5

Rising interest rates are probably boosting the net interest margins of the
large banks. However, the rapid ascent in bond yields in recent weeks has
unnerved investors, as evidenced by the sharp drop in the sector's
forward P/E since mid-August (chart). Bond yields may be stabilizing as
investors are drawn to higher returns.
The sector's forward earnings is up 17.3% y/y, while its stock price index
is up only 4.9% y/y (chart). A solid Q3 earnings season should lift the

sector.

### Page 6

So far this year, insurance industries have performed best in the sector
(chart).
(3) Industrials. Industrials balanced competing forces this week—
benefiting from safe-haven rotation and robust structural demand, while
facing occasional headwinds from surging energy input costs and
persistent high-interest-rate pressures. This S&P 500 sector is also testing
support at its 200-day moving average (chart).

### Page 7

This is another sector where forward earnings is rising faster than the
sector's stock price index (chart). The price action sometimes leads the
growth rate in forward earnings. This time may be different as the AI
buildout continues to boost the sector's earnings.
Construction Machinery & Heavy Trucks is the sector's best-performing
industry so far this year (chart). Second best has been Rail
Transportation. Notwithstanding the wars in the Middle East and between

Russia and Ukraine, Aerospace & Defense has been the weakest
industry. Military budgets for large munitions and battlefield weapons may
have to make room for more spending on AI-guided drones and
"Terminators."

### Page 8

clause.

## Document 299: The Commodities Feed_ Oil holds firm as Middle East breakthrough remains elusive

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Commodities Feed_ Oil holds firm as Middle East breakthrough remains elusive.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Oil is holding ﬁrm as Middle East tensions refuse to cool.
Meanwhile, IEA members agreed to release the rest of the
emergency stockpiles they pledged earlier this year

Warren Patterson and Ewa Manthey
Energy – IEA member to release remaining
oil
Oil remains well-supported. A pattern has set in: prices slide early in
the session, then claw back most of those losses by the close. As a
result, ICE Brent continues to comfortably trade above $100/bbl.
Uncertainty in the Middle East and concerns over the sustainability
of oil ﬂows through the Strait of Hormuz has the market pricing in a
signiﬁcant risk premium. The longer this pickup in ﬂows continues,
the more likely the market will start giving back some of this risk
premium. In order to see a full removal, there needs to be some kind
of agreement between the US and Iran. This would provide the
market with more conﬁdence that stronger oil ﬂows from the region
will continue.
Cookie settings

### Page 2

International Energy Agency members agreed to release the
remaining 100m barrels of oil from strategic reserves. It's part of the
initial 400m barrels that members agreed back in March to help
with the Persian Gulf supply shortfall. According to the IEA,
members have distributed around 330m barrels to date. From the
100m barrels to be released, 20m barrels will be diesel, and will
make its way onto the market as soon as possible. The US probably
won’t be happy with the scale of the release, particularly regarding

diesel. Initial suggestions were that we could see around 50m
barrels of diesel released. The risk is that if the US believes these
volumes are not enough, the administration may mull restrictions
on US diesel exports once again.
The Financial Times reports that the US is considering easing
sanctions on Russia’s shadow tanker ﬂeet in bid to reduce fuel
costs. Tanker freight rates surged to record levels recently due to the
growing ineﬃciency in trade ﬂows out of the Persian Gulf. Since the

start of the Russia-Ukraine war, Russia has built a signiﬁcant ﬂeet of
tankers, which are largely sanctioned. The idea is that easing
sanctions on these vessels would free up tanker capacity for the
broader market. The key question is how eﬀective it might be in
bringing down spot tanker rates. It’s unclear how much of this
capacity is actually available. Given UK and EU sanctions, insurance
could still be an issue.
The latest data from the EIA shows that US commercial crude oil

inventories fell by 8.56m barrels over the last week, which is the
largest weekly decline since February. Hurricane activity in the Gulf
played part in the inventory decline, with oﬀshore production shut
in due to the storm. US output is estimated to have fallen by 300k
b/d week-on-week. Crude oil imports fell by 791k b/d WoW, a key
driver behind the fall in stocks. For reﬁned products, US reﬁneries
increased their utilisation rates by 0.8pp to 93.5%, leaving

utilisation rates at their highest seasonal levels in at least 20 years.
Despite this, distillate fuel stocks still fell by 2.53m barrels over the
last week. This leaves distillate inventories at record seasonal lows
as we head into winter. Meanwhile, gasoline stocks increased by
3.52m barrels.

### Page 3

Metals — Iron ore breaks below $90…
brieﬂy
Iron ore prices fell brieﬂy below $90/t yesterday, the lowest since
November 2022, as weak Chinese demand and abundant global
supply continued to weigh on sentiment. Prices have declined more
than 14% this year, pressured by the prolonged weakness in
China’s property and construction sectors. This is undermining
steel demand. China’s crude steel production during the ﬁrst eight
months of 2026 fell to its lowest level for the period since 2018,

highlighting the impact of the country’s real estate downturn. With
steel mills operating on thin margins, further production cuts
remain possible. Additional supply entering the market has kept
iron ore fundamentals well supplied. As a result, prices are expected
to remain rangebound, with near-term risks skewed to the
downside.
Steel inventories at major Chinese mills rose to 17mt in early
October, up 8.4% from late September, according to CISA data.

Inventories were 6.9% higher than a year earlier, indicating
subdued underlying demand. Meanwhile, crude steel production at
major mills increased 3.1% from late September to 1.94mt/d in
early October.
Agriculture —Cocoa demand recovery
support prices
Cocoa prices ﬁrmed yesterday, with London cocoa settling a little
more than 1% higher amid ﬁrmer demand numbers. Data from the
European Cocoa Association showed European cocoa grindings
rose 3.2% year-on-year and 10% quarter-on-quarter to 348.2kt in

3Q26, marking the ﬁrst annual increase since 2Q24. Similarly, the
Cocoa Association of Asia reported a 29.2% YoY increase in
grindings and a 4.1% QoQ rise to a record 233.8kt.
In its monthly update, the International Grains Council (IGC) raised
its 2026/27 global corn production forecast to 1,307mt from
1,301mt, while consumption was revised up to 1,323mt from
1,321mt. This saw global corn ending stocks increase to 297mt

## Document 336: Westpac Card Tracker, 2 October 2026 (2026-10-02)

### Page 1

WESTPAC–DATAX
CARD TRACKER
2 OCTOBER 2026
Powered by

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
2
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
• The Westpac–DataX Card Tracker
Index* has edged up slightly since late
August, rising 0.2pts to 159.1.
• Quarterly growth momentum has
seen a further lift and is now tracking
towards a 1.7% gain for the September
quarter which would mark the strongest

quarterly rise since June 2025.
• However, both the monthly and weekly
reads show momentum has faded over
the course of the quarter. After posting
0.7-0.9%mth gains through May, June
and July, the Index rose just 0.2%
in August and is tracking a similarly
subdued 0.3% pace for September.
Weekly reads are also tracking a more
moderate trend pace of 0.2%mth.
• On a quarterly basis, the detail shows
discretionary services and international
transactions have led the pick-up.

While some of this looks to be catch-
up from travel disruptions in the June
quarter, gains have been consistent and
broadly based. Likewise, there is little
evidence that the pick-up is coming from
a lift in prices with recent CPI updates
suggesting services inflation has been
steady rather than accelerating (see
here).
• On the apparent loss of momentum
over the course of the quarter, the detail
suggests this is a re-run of the fuel price
squeeze in March-April-May with the

essentials spend gain rising strongly. So
far the spillover effects are showing up
mainly as a dampening in discretionary
goods spend with the slowing in
discretionary services largely confined to
hospitality.
• Overall, the card tracker data suggests
the Q3 spending data will still come in
as a relatively solid gain, even allowing
for a firmer gain in prices in the quarter.
However, the fade in momentum is
notable with the impact of further fuel

pricer increases and this week’s RBA rate
rise still to come. Add what now looks
more likely to be a follow-on interest
rate hike in November and the December
quarter is shaping as a significant
slowdown in card activity.
“... both the monthly
and weekly reads show
momentum has faded over
the course of the quarter.”

rise in nominal terms and price rise in the

1%qtr range points to a real, inflation
adjusted gain of around 0.7%qtr, in line
with Westpac’s current forecasts.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS 9
About the Westpac card data indicators
The indicators presented in this report are based on the millions of credit and debit card
transactions processed every day. Transactions covering over ten million merchants are
classified into over 700 categories. These are in turn grouped into higher level aggregates

that provide a timely guide to wider economic trends.
The main focus of these indicators is consumer spending. Where possible, we have sought
to exclude ‘non spending’ transactions such as: money transfers; tax payments; loan
repayments; charitable donations; and superannuation contributions.
It should also be noted that these indicators will also be affected by shifts between card and
non-card transactions. This was a significant factor during the COVID–19 pandemic – health

concerns about the use of physical cash leading to significantly higher use of cards vs cash,
particularly where contact–less transactions are available. Transaction flows also include
reversals/refunds which were also a significant phenomenon during the onset of COVID,
especially in areas such as travel.
All transaction data is compiled at a highly aggregated level so that individual customer or
merchant data is never revealed.
Index construction

The key metrics used in this report are indexes of spending–related card activity where the
base of 100 is average activity in 2019. As an example, if transaction flows are 5% above their
average level in 2019, the index read for the period is 105. If flows in a subsequent period are
8% above the average level in 2019, the index read for this period is 108. Growth between the
two periods can be calculated simply as the change between the two index reads, i.e. 2.9%.

All measures are adjusted for regular seasonality. Weekly estimates are generated using
the US Bureau of Labor’s MoveReg weekly seasonal adjustment program. Note that in some
cases, high levels of volatility during the COVID period mean it is not possible to produce
seasonally adjusted estimates for some historical periods.
Also, note that previous versions of this report used different approaches to seasonal
adjustment and measurement more generally. This means Index reads are sometimes not

directly comparable. See the ‘About the Westpac card data indicators’ sections from earlier
reports to more detail.
Classifications
Note that the measures used for card data and in this report do not align completely with
the those used in official ABS statistics, including the ABS household spending indicator,
ABS retail trade survey and ABS estimate of consumer spending published in the national
accounts. There are a range of differences including around both coverage and classification.

As such, the card data should be treated as broadly indicative.
The transaction data is grouped into 26 categories that are then combined into four main as
follows:
Discretionary goods: alcoholic beverages, tobacco, clothing & footwear, furnishings &
household equipment, household appliances, vehicle–related, recreational & cultural goods,
newspaper, books & stationery, and other personal effects.
Discretionary services: transport services (part), recreational & cultural services, gambling,

catering services, accommodation services, other personal care, insurance & financial
services, other services.
Essential goods: food & non alcoholic beverages, medical products, appliances & equipment,
and operation of personal transport equipment.
Essential services: electricity, gas & other fuels, health services, transport services (part),
communications and education.
The report also uses two additional classifications:
MHSI/Retail: based on the extent to which categories cover activity that is in scope for the

ABS monthly household spending indicator and ABS retail trade survey.
